Morse theoretic signal compression and reconstruction on chain complexes
Authors: Stefania Ebli, Celia Hacker, Kelly Maggs
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
At the intersection of Topological Data Analysis (TDA) and machine learning, the field of cellular signal processing has advanced rapidly in recent years. In this context, each signal on the cells of a complex is processed using the combinatorial Laplacian, and the resultant Hodge decomposition. Meanwhile, discrete Morse theory has been widely used to speed up computations by reducing the size of complexes while preserving their global topological properties. In this paper, we provide an approach to signal compression and reconstruction on chain complexes that leverages the tools of algebraic discrete Morse theory. The main goal is to reduce and reconstruct a based chain complex together with a set of signals on its cells via deformation retracts, preserving as much as possible the global topological structure of both the complex and the signals. We first prove that any deformation retract of real degree-wise finite-dimensional based chain complexes is equivalent to a Morse matching. We will then study how the signal changes under particular types of Morse matchings, showing its reconstruction error is trivial on specific components of the Hodge decomposition. Furthermore, we provide an algorithm to compute Morse matchings with minimal reconstruction error.

Introduction

The analysis of signals supported on topological objects such as graphs or simplicial complexes is a fast-growing field combining techniques from topological data analysis, machine learning and signal processing (Barbarossa et al. 2018; Ortega et al. 2018; Robinson 2014). The emerging field of simplicial and cellular signal processing falls within this paradigm (Barbarossa and Sardellitti 2020; Schaub et al. 2021; Roddenberry et al. 2022), and here the combinatorial Laplacian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _n$$\end{document}Δn plays a pivotal role. In this context, a signal takes the form of a real-valued chain (or cochain) on a chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},\partial )$$\end{document}(C,∂) endowed with a degree-wise inner product. In particular, the eigenvectors of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _n$$\end{document}Δn, called the Hodge basis, serve as a ‘topological’ Fourier basis to transform a signal into a topologically meaningful coordinate system (Ebli et al. 2020; Schaub et al. 2021). Additionally, the combinatorial Laplacian gives rise to the combinatorial Hodge decomposition (Eckmann 1944):\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{C}_n = \textrm{Im}\,\partial _{n+1} \oplus {{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\Delta _n \oplus \textrm{Im}\,\partial _n^\dagger , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cn=Im∂n+1⊕KerΔn⊕Im∂n†,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial ^\dagger _n$$\end{document}∂n† is the adjoint of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial $$\end{document}∂ with respect to the given inner product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}Cn. The components of the Hodge decomposition each have their own topological interpretation (Barbarossa and Sardellitti 2020) and respect the eigendecomposition of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _n$$\end{document}Δn. This Fourier representation has proven to be useful in multiple applications (Barbarossa et al. 2018; Ortega et al. 2018). In graph signal processing, it has been exploited for signal smoothing and denoising (Chen et al. 2014; Zhou and Schölkopf 2004), node embeddings via Laplacian eigenmaps (Belkin and Niyogi 2003; Luxburg 2007), graph neural networks (Bronstein et al. 2017; Defferrard et al. 2016), and signal compression and reconstruction (Li et al. 2021).

The compression and reconstruction of data or signals is a vast field in data science. This typically involves two stages: first, compressing high-dimensional data into a smaller space, and secondly, reconstructing or recovering the data in its original space. One example of this is image compression, which utilizes compression algorithms to reduce the number of pixels while simultaneously mapping their values onto a smaller domain. Further, there exist algorithms to reconstruct high-resolution images from their compressed counterparts.

The goal of the paper is to investigate and define signal compression and reconstruction over cell complexes by combining tools of Hodge theory and discrete Morse theory. In this context, a signal is an element of the (co)chain complex. In analogy to image compression and reconstruction, in the world of cell complexes we wish to compress cell complexes equipped with signals by reducing the size of the complex, while preserving a general structure and mapping the signal of the cells to the new cell complex in an appropriate way. For us the structure to preserve is the homology of the underlying complex, which can be done by Morse collapses and this method also gives a way of mapping the signal value to the smaller cell complex.

We take an entirely algebraic approach to this problem, working at the level of degree-wise finite-dimensional based chain complexes endowed with inner products. The classical example is the chain complex of a cell complex equipped with its canonical cellular basis, but more general constructions such as cellular sheaves fit into this framework as well. This algebraic perspective not only gives us greater flexibility, but also helps to illuminate connections between Hodge theory and discrete Morse theory that occur only at the level of chain complexes.

Our approach to compressing and reconstructing signals over complexes involves deformation retracts of based chain complexes, which have the advantage of reducing the size of complexes while preserving their homology. A deformation retract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi , \Phi , h)$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h) of a chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C onto \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}$$\end{document}D consists of a pair of chain maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \Phi = {{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}_{\textbf{D}}$$\end{document}ΨΦ=IdD and a chain homotopy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h: \textbf{C}\rightarrow \textbf{C}$$\end{document}h:C→C between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} In this context, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is used to compress the signal s onto the reduced complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}$$\end{document}D, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ serves to reconstruct it back in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C. Thus, for every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}s∈C one can compute the difference \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \Psi s -s$$\end{document}ΦΨs-s, called the reconstruction error, to understand and evaluate how compression and reconstruction changes the signal.

Among the many topological methods to reduce the size of complexes (Singh et al. 2007; Wood et al. 2011), discrete Morse theory (Forman 1998, 2002) provides the perfect tool to efficiently generate such deformation retracts of chain complexes. This technique has already been used with great success in the compression of 3D images (Wood et al. 2011), persistent homology (Mischaikow 2013) and cellular sheaves (Curry et al. 2016). In this paper we utilise Sköldberg’s algebraic version of discrete Morse theory (Sköldberg 2018, 2006). It takes as input a based chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C and, by reducing its based structure with respect to a Morse matching M, returns a smaller, chain-equivalent complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}^M$$\end{document}CM. The first result presented in this article connects the Hodge decomposition of a complex with discrete Morse theory by defining a natural pairing in the Hodge basis. In particular, we show that any deformation retract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi ,h)$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h) of degree-wise finite-dimensional, based chain complexes of real inner product spaces can be obtained from a Morse matching over the Hodge basis of a certain sub-complex. This process, called the Morsification of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi ,h)$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h), is described in Theorem 3.7. In the second part of the paper, we study how the reconstruction error associated to a deformation retract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi ,h)$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h) is distributed amongst the three components of the Hodge decomposition. We define a class of deformation retracts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi , h )$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h), called \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n,n-1)$$\end{document}(n,n-1)-free, for which the reconstruction error has trivial (co)cycle reconstruction. Specifically, they are characterised by the following properties (Theorem 4.5). (Cocycle Reconstruction) A signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}s∈Cn and its reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \Psi s$$\end{document}ΦΨs encode the same cocycle information: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Proj}_{{{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\partial _{n+1}^\dagger } (\Phi \Psi s - s) = 0\, \hbox {for all}\, s \in \textbf{C}_n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ProjKer∂n+1†(ΦΨs-s)=0for alls∈Cn.(Cycle Reconstruction) A signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_{n-1}$$\end{document}s∈Cn-1 and the adjoint of the reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ^\dagger \Phi ^\dagger s$$\end{document}Ψ†Φ†s have the same cycle information: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Proj}_{{{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\partial _{n-1}} (\Psi ^\dagger \Phi ^\dagger s - (Cocycle Reconstruction) A signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}s∈Cn and its reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \Psi s$$\end{document}ΦΨs encode the same cocycle information: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Proj}_{{{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\partial _{n+1}^\dagger } (\Phi \Psi s - s) = 0\, \hbox {for all}\, s \in \textbf{C}_n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ProjKer∂n+1†(ΦΨs-s)=0for alls∈Cn.

(Cocycle Reconstruction) A signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}s∈Cn and its reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \Psi s$$\end{document}ΦΨs encode the same cocycle information: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Proj}_{{{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\partial _{n+1}^\dagger } (\Phi \Psi s - s) = 0\, \hbox {for all}\, s \in \textbf{C}_n. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ProjKer∂n+1†(ΦΨs-s)=0for alls∈Cn.

(Cycle Reconstruction) A signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_{n-1}$$\end{document}s∈Cn-1 and the adjoint of the reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi ^\dagger \Phi ^\dagger s$$\end{document}Ψ†Φ†s have the same cycle information: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Proj}_{{{\,\textrm{Ker}\,}}\partial _{n-1}} (\Psi ^\dagger \Phi ^\dagger s - s) = 0\, \hbox {for all}\, s \in \textbf{C}_{n-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ProjKer∂n-1(Ψ†Φ†s-s)=0for alls∈Cn-1.

Finally, we study how the reconstruction error of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n,n-1)$$\end{document}(n,n-1)-free deformation retracts can be minimized while maintaining (co)cycle reconstruction. We develop an iterative algorithm to find the retract \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi )$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ) that minimizes the norm of the reconstruction error for a given signal \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s\in \textbf{C}$$\end{document}s∈C. Our algorithm is inspired by the reduction pair algorithms in Curry et al. (2016), Kaczyński et al. (1998), Mischaikow (2013) and, like these algorithms, computes a single Morse matching at each step with the additional requirement of minimizing the norm. We show that its computational complexity is linear when the complex is sparse, and discuss bounds on how well the iterative process approximates the optimal deformation retract. Finally, we show computationally that iterating single optimal collapses leads to reconstruction loss that is significantly lower than that arising from performing sequences of random collapses.

The paper is structured as follows. In Sect. 2, we present the necessary background in algebraic topology, discrete Hodge theory, and algebraic discrete Morse theory, giving the definitions and main results that will be used throughout the paper. Section 3 introduces the notion of Hodge matching, which allows us to prove that every deformation retract of a degree-wise finite-dimensional based chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C of real inner product spaces is equivalent to a Morse retract (see Morsification Theorem 3.7). In Sect. 4 we investigate the interaction between deformation retracts and Hodge theory. The main results, Theorem 4.5 and Corollary 4.6, utilise the Morsification theorem to prove that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n,n-1)$$\end{document}(n,n-1)-free (sequential) Morse matchings preserve (co)cycles. Section 4.3 presents an additional result that explains how the reconstruction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \Psi s$$\end{document}ΦΨs can be understood as a sparsification of the signal s (see Lemma 4.10). Finally, Sect. 5 is dedicated to presenting algorithms to minimize the reconstruction error in case of iterative single pairings (see Algorithms 1 and 2).

Related work

Many articles incorporate topology into the loss or reconstruction error function (Carrière et al. 2020; Gabrielsson et al. 2020; Kim et al. 2020; Moor et al. 2020), however, these deal almost exclusively with point cloud data. At the same time discrete Morse theory has been used in conjunction with machine learning in Hu et al. (2021) for image processing, but not in the context of reconstruction error optimisation.

In Forman (2002), the concept of taking duals (over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z) of discrete Morse theoretic constructions is introduced using a dual flow. However, in our approach, we use an adjoint flow over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R, which enables us to establish a connection to Hodge theory. This connection is not feasible when working solely over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z, as the use of inner products is necessary.

On the computational side, the articles (Curry et al. 2016; Kaczyński et al. 1998; Kaczynski 2006; Mischaikow 2013) involve algorithms to reduce chain complexes over arbitrary principal ideal domains, including those of cellular sheaves but do not investigate the connection with the combinatorial Laplacian (or sheaf Laplacian). Our algorithms are based on the coreduction algorithms of (Kaczynski 2006; Kaczyński et al. 1998), with the additional requirement of a loss minimization.

Numerous studies have been conducted to explore the relationship between persistent homology and discrete Morse theory (Delgado-Friedrichs et al. 2015; Du et al. 2018; Mischaikow 2013; Wood et al. 2011). As far as we are aware, there are only two other recent studies, namely (Contreras and Tawfeek 2021) and (Contreras 2019), that investigate the connection between the combinatorial Hodge decomposition and discrete Morse theory. The former links the coefficients of the characteristic equation of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _n$$\end{document}Δn to the n-dimensional paths in an acyclic partial matching, while the latter studies the effect of a discrete Morse function on a deformed version of the Laplacians in dimensions 0 and 1.

Background

In this section, for the sake of completeness, we first recall some basic notions in algebraic topology. We refer the reader to Hatcher (2002) for a more detailed exposition. Then we present the main concepts of algebraic discrete Morse theory and finally, we discuss the foundations of discrete Hodge theory.

Algebraic discrete Morse theory

For two chain complexes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},\partial )$$\end{document}(C,∂) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{D},\partial ')$$\end{document}(D,∂′), a pair of chain maps \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi :\textbf{C}\rightarrow \textbf{D}$$\end{document}Ψ:C→D and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi :\textbf{D}\rightarrow \textbf{C}$$\end{document}Φ:D→C are chain equivalances if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi \circ \Psi : \textbf{C}\rightarrow \textbf{C}$$\end{document}Φ∘Ψ:C→C and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \circ \Phi : \textbf{D} \rightarrow \textbf{D}$$\end{document}Ψ∘Φ:D→D are chain homotopic to the identities on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}$$\end{document}D, respectively. Note that this implies that the maps induced on the homology modules by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ are isomorphisms. The chain equivalences \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ form a deformation retract of the chain complexes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}$$\end{document}D if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi \circ \Phi $$\end{document}Ψ∘Φ is the identity map on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{D}$$\end{document}D. Deformation retracts will be often depicted as the following diagram. With a slight abuse of notation, we denote such deformation retract by the pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi )$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ) instead of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Psi ,\Phi ,h)$$\end{document}(Ψ,Φ,h). Throughout the paper we will be working with the following notion of based chain complexes, as defined in Sköldberg (2018), which in this context are chain complexes with a graded structure.

Definition 2.1

Let R be a commutative ring. A based chain complex of R-modules is a pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I), where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C is a chain complex of R-modules and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I= \{I_n\}_{n\in \mathbb {N}}$$\end{document}I={In}n∈N is a set of mutually disjoint sets such that for all n and all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in I_n$$\end{document}α∈In there exist \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha \subseteq \textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}Cα⊆Cn such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}_n = \bigoplus _{\alpha \in I_n} C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cn=⨁α∈InCα.

The components of the boundary operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _n$$\end{document}∂n are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta , \alpha }: C_\alpha \rightarrow C_\beta $$\end{document}∂β,α:Cα→Cβ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in I_n$$\end{document}α∈In and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta \in I_{n-1}$$\end{document}β∈In-1. We will refer to the elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_n$$\end{document}In as the n-cells of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I), and if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta , \alpha } \ne 0$$\end{document}∂β,α≠0, we say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β is a face of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C is endowed with a degree-wise inner product, we say that I is an orthogonal base if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha \perp C_{\beta }$$\end{document}Cα⊥Cβ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \ne \beta \in I$$\end{document}α≠β∈I. Similarly, a based cochain complex is a cochain complex with an indexing set and graded decomposition as above.

Remark 2.2

We would like to draw attention to the fact that the notion of base in the definition of based chain complexes above, and originally defined in Sköldberg (2018), differs from the notion of basis in the following way. The choice of base is an additional structure providing a grading, or a direct sum decomposition, of each chain group. This does not necessarily coincide with a choice of basis, since base elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα need not be one-dimensional (See Example 2.6).

The advantage of introducing the notion of base lies in the fact that this allow us to define algebraic Morse matchings (Definition 2.8) and the subsequent deformation retracts given in Theorem 2.9. The framework of these based chain complexes encapsulate not only standard chain complexes generated from cell complexes, but also cellular sheaves as well as purely algebraic chain complexes when no cell structure is specified.

Remark 2.3

In this paper, working with combinatorial Hodge theory means that, if not specified otherwise, we restrict our study to degree-wise finite-dimensional chain complexes over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R with an inner product on each of the chain module \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}Cn.1 Moreover, we will refer to degree-wise finite-dimensional based chain complexes as finite-type based chain complexes.

The following examples motivate such a choice of terminology for based chain complexes.

Example 2.4

In the special case where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I) is a finite-type based chain complex over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}R and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha \cong \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}Cα≅R for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in I$$\end{document}α∈I, we can think of I as a choice of basis, and each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta ,\alpha } \in \textsf{Hom}(\mathbb {R},\mathbb {R}) = \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}∂β,α∈Hom(R,R)=R as the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\beta ,\alpha )$$\end{document}(β,α)-entry in the boundary matrix multiplying on the left with respect to such a basis.

Example 2.5

(CW complexes) The chain complex associated to a finite CW complex with a basis given by its cells is an example of a based chain complex (see Hatcher 2002 for a precise definition of CW complex). In this case, the basis and the base coincide. For two cells \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} Example 2.6

(Cellular Sheaves) Here we present the main definitions for cellular sheaves, following the more detailed exposition of sheaf Laplacians found in Hansen and Ghrist (2019). Using the inner product on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}Cn induced by the inner product on each Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {F}(\sigma )$$\end{document}F(σ), one can define a boundary map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _n: \textbf{C}_{n+1} \rightarrow \textbf{C}_{n} $$\end{document}∂n:Cn+1→Cn as the adjoint of the coboundary map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\delta _n$$\end{document}δn. This chain complex is an example of a based chain complex, where the n-cells of the base correspond the n-cells of the underlying indexing complex. However, in this case the base does not correspond to a basis since the spaces corresponding to each base element are not one-dimensional.

Discrete Morse theory was originally introduced by Forman in Forman (1998) as a combinatorial version of classical Morse theory. Here we present its fundamental ideas in a purely algebraic setting, following the exposition in Sköldberg (2018).

Definition 2.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I) be a finite-type based chain complex with base I. We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}G(C,I) the graph of the complex, which is the directed graph consisting of vertices I and edges \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow \beta $$\end{document}α→β whenever \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta ,\alpha }$$\end{document}∂β,α is non-zero. When clear from the context we will denote \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}G(C,I) by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})$$\end{document}G(C). For a subset of edges E of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})$$\end{document}G(C), denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})^E$$\end{document}G(C)E the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})$$\end{document}G(C) with the edges of E reversed.

Using these notions we can define a Morse matching as follows.

Definition 2.8

An (algebraic) Morse matching M on a based complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} {G}(\textbf{C})^M$$\end{document}G(C)M is a partial order.

each vertex in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})$$\end{document}G(C) is adjacent to at most one edge in M;

each vertex in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})$$\end{document}G(C) is adjacent to at most one edge in M;

for each edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow \beta $$\end{document}α→β in M, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta , \alpha }$$\end{document}∂β,α is an isomorphism;

for each edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow \beta $$\end{document}α→β in M, the map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\partial _{\beta , \alpha }$$\end{document}∂β,α is an isomorphism;

the relation on each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_n$$\end{document}In given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \succ \beta $$\end{document}α≻β whenever there exists a directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})^M$$\end{document}G(C)M is a partial order.

the relation on each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_n$$\end{document}In given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \succ \beta $$\end{document}α≻β whenever there exists a directed path from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C})^M$$\end{document}G(C)M is a partial order.

For context, the third condition corresponds to acyclicity in the classical Morse matching definition, where directed paths akin to gradient flow-lines – which are non-periodic – in the smooth Morse theory setting (Milnor 1969).

When there is an edge \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow \beta $$\end{document}α→β in M, we say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β are paired in M, and refer to them as a \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\dim \alpha , \dim \alpha -1)$$\end{document}(dimα,dimα-1)-pairing. We use \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^0$$\end{document}M0 to denote the elements of I that are not paired by M, and refer to them as critical cells of the pairing. For a directed path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma = \alpha , \sigma _1, \ldots , \sigma _k, \beta $$\end{document}γ=α,σ1,…,σk,β in the graph \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}(\textbf{C},I)^M$$\end{document}G(C,I)M, the index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}(\gamma )$$\end{document}I(γ) of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ is then defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {I}(\gamma ) = \epsilon _n \partial _{\beta , \sigma _n}^{\epsilon _n} \circ \ldots \circ \epsilon _1 \partial _{\sigma _2, \sigma _1}^{\epsilon _1} \circ \epsilon _0 \partial _{\sigma _1, \alpha }^{\epsilon _0}: C_\alpha \rightarrow C_\beta \end{aligned}$$\end{document}I(γ)=ϵn∂β,σnn∘…∘ϵ1∂σ2,σ11∘ϵ0∂σ1,α0:Cα→Cβwhere \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _i = -1$$\end{document}ϵi=-1 if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _i \rightarrow \sigma _{i+1}$$\end{document}σi→σi+1 is an element of M, and 1 otherwise. Note that the index is not defined in the case of the trivial path where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha = \beta $$\end{document}α=β. For any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha , \beta \in I$$\end{document}α,β∈I, we define the summed index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{\alpha , \beta }$$\end{document}Γα,β to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Gamma _{\beta ,\alpha } = \sum _{\gamma : \alpha \rightarrow \beta } \mathcal {I}(\gamma ): C_\alpha \rightarrow C_\beta , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Γβ,α=∑γ:α→βI(γ):Cα→Cβ,the sum over all possible non-trivial paths from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha $$\end{document}α to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\beta $$\end{document}β. In the case that there are no paths from \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \rightarrow \beta $$\end{document}α→β then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{\beta ,\alpha } = 0$$\end{document}Γβ,α=0.

The theorem below is the main theorem of algebraic Morse theory. While this theorem was originally proved in Sköldberg (2006), here we state it in the form presented in Sköldberg (2018) where it is proved as a corollary of the Homological Perturbation Lemma ( Sköldberg 2018, Theorem 1, Brown 1965; Gugenheim 1972). This proof provides an explicit description of the chain homotopy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$h:\textbf{C}\rightarrow \textbf{C}$$\end{document}h:C→C that witnesses the fact that the algebraic Morse reduction is a homotopy equivalence.

Theorem 2.9

(Sköldberg 2018) Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I) be a based chain complex indexed by I, and M a Morse matching. For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n \ge 0$$\end{document}n≥0 let\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textbf{C}^M_n = \bigoplus _{\alpha \in I_n \cap M^0} C_\alpha . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CnM=⨁α∈In∩M0Cα.The diagram where for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in M^0 \cap I_n$$\end{document}α∈M0∩In and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x \in C_\alpha $$\end{document}x∈Cα\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \partial _{\textbf{C}^M}(x)&= \sum _{\beta \in M^0 \cap I_{n-1}} \Gamma _{\beta ,\alpha }(x) \hspace{3em} \Phi (x)&= \sum _{\beta \in I_n } \Gamma _{\beta ,\alpha }(x) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂CM(x)=∑β∈M0∩In-1Γβ,α(x)Φ(x)=∑β∈InΓβ,α(x)and for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in I_n$$\end{document}α∈In and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \begin{aligned} \Psi (x)&= \sum _{\beta \in M^0 \cap I_{n}} \Gamma _{\beta , \alpha }(x) \qquad h(x)&= \sum _{\beta \in I_{n+1}} \Gamma _{\beta ,\alpha }(x) \end{aligned} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ(x)=∑β∈M0∩InΓβ,α(x)h(x)=∑β∈In+1Γβ,α(x)is a deformation retract4 of chain complexes.

We refer to the finite-type based chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C}^M,\partial _{C^M}, I \cap M^0)$$\end{document}(CM,∂CM,I∩M0) as the Morse chain complex. Moreover, we call this deformation retract of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C into \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}^M$$\end{document}CM the Morse retract induced by M.

Example 2.10

Given a based chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C},I)$$\end{document}(C,I) and a single \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+1,n)$$\end{document}(n+1,n)-pairing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M = (\alpha \rightarrow \beta )$$\end{document}M=(α→β), Lemma 2.9 can be used to get a simple closed form of the Morse chain complex \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C}^M, \partial _{\textbf{C}^M})$$\end{document}(CM,∂CM), as well as the chain equivalences. We write them explicitly here, and will refer to them throughout the paper.For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau , \sigma \in M^0$$\end{document}τ,σ∈M0, the Morse boundary operator is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial ^{\textbf{C}^M}_{\tau ,\sigma } = \partial _{\tau ,\sigma } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\sigma }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τ,σM=∂τ,σ-∂τ,α∂β,α-1∂β,σ.The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\beta $$\end{document}Cβ, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n}^M\big |_{C_\beta }} = \sum _{\tau \in I_{n} \setminus \beta } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \hspace{5em} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n+1}^M\big |_{C_\alpha }} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨnM|Cβ=∑τ∈In\β-∂τ,α∂β,α-1Ψn+1M|Cα=0.The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\eta $$\end{document}Cη for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in M^0 \cap I_{n+1}$$\end{document}η∈M0∩In+1, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Phi ^M_{n+1}\big |_{C_\eta }}={{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}_{C_\eta } -\partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\eta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φn+1M|Cη=IdCη-∂β,α-1∂β,η.Note that these equations are identical to those appearing in Kaczyński et al. (1998), Mischaikow (2013) in the case that each component \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα is of dimension 1.

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau , \sigma \in M^0$$\end{document}τ,σ∈M0, the Morse boundary operator is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial ^{\textbf{C}^M}_{\tau ,\sigma } = \partial _{\tau ,\sigma } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\sigma }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τ,σM=∂τ,σ-∂τ,α∂β,α-1∂β,σ.

For every \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau , \sigma \in M^0$$\end{document}τ,σ∈M0, the Morse boundary operator is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \partial ^{\textbf{C}^M}_{\tau ,\sigma } = \partial _{\tau ,\sigma } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\sigma }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∂τ,σM=∂τ,σ-∂τ,α∂β,α-1∂β,σ.

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\beta $$\end{document}Cβ, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n}^M\big |_{C_\beta }} = \sum _{\tau \in I_{n} \setminus \beta } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \hspace{5em} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n+1}^M\big |_{C_\alpha }} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨnM|Cβ=∑τ∈In\β-∂τ,α∂β,α-1Ψn+1M|Cα=0.

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi $$\end{document}Ψ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\alpha $$\end{document}Cα and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\beta $$\end{document}Cβ, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n}^M\big |_{C_\beta }} = \sum _{\tau \in I_{n} \setminus \beta } - \partial _{\tau ,\alpha } \partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \hspace{5em} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Psi _{n+1}^M\big |_{C_\alpha }} = 0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ΨnM|Cβ=∑τ∈In\β-∂τ,α∂β,α-1Ψn+1M|Cα=0.

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\eta $$\end{document}Cη for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in M^0 \cap I_{n+1}$$\end{document}η∈M0∩In+1, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Phi ^M_{n+1}\big |_{C_\eta }}={{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}_{C_\eta } -\partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\eta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φn+1M|Cη=IdCη-∂β,α-1∂β,η.

The map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi $$\end{document}Φ is the identity except at components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_\eta $$\end{document}Cη for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta \in M^0 \cap I_{n+1}$$\end{document}η∈M0∩In+1, where it is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\hspace{0.0pt}\Phi ^M_{n+1}\big |_{C_\eta }}={{\,\textrm{Id}\,}}_{C_\eta } -\partial _{\beta ,\alpha }^{-1} \partial _{\beta ,\eta }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φn+1M|Cη=IdCη-∂β,α-1∂β,η.

When \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textbf{C}, I)$$\end{document}(C,I) is a finite-type based chain complex of real inner product spaces, the adjoints of the maps in Theorem 2.9 play an important role in later sections. Their discrete Morse theoretic interpretation in terms of flow, however, hinges on the orthogonality of the base of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{C}$$\end{document}C (see Appendix A.2). We will require the following basic result of linear algebra regarding adjoints throughout the paper.

Lemma 2.11

Let V be an finite dimensional inner product space and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W \subseteq V$$\end{document}W⊆V be a subspace. The adjoint of the inclusion map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i: W \rightarrow V$$\end{document}i:W→V is the orthogonal projection \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{Proj}_W = i^\dag $$\end{document}ProjW=i† onto W.

Remark 2.13

Motivated by the emerging field of cellular signal processing, we refer to elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$s \in \textbf{C}_n$$\end{document}s∈Cn as signals ( Barbarossa and Sardellitti 2020; Schaub et al. 2021).

In the next definition we introduce the concept of sequential Morse matching, an iterative sequence of Morse matchings. This type of matching, unlike a Morse matching, has a low computational cost to reduce the chain complex to a minimal number of critical cells. We discuss this in detail in Sect. 5, where we leverage the sequential nature of these matchings to provide efficient algorithms to minimise the reconstruction error.
